# ERP MVP PLAN — Indian Manufacturing SMEs

**Status:** decided on architecture, **not costed**. This is the deliverable of the six-phase scout (PHASE1–PHASE6, same directory). Every load-bearing claim cites its report. Where reports disagreed, the conflict is named and resolved inline. Bets are labelled **[BET]**; unverified statutory data is labelled **[VERIFY]**.

**Revision 5** — revised after two adversarial review rounds (skeptical CTO, Indian CA, implementing engineer) and **two** market-research passes (`PHASE7-MARKET-RESEARCH.md`, then the deeper `PHASE8-MARKET-RESEARCH-DEEP.md`, which supersedes PHASE7 wherever they conflict). See §11 for the material corrections of each round, §11's Revision-4 subsection for the Phase 7 merge, and **§11's Revision-5 subsection for the Phase 8 merge and the two edits it declined**. The discipline those rounds enforced, and that now governs this document: *every statutory assertion carries an evidence label and a §5.5 question number, or it is a defect; every DDL fragment must be valid Postgres; and every capability named in a wedge must appear in a stage.* Claims that read as settled law but were the scout's inference or the author's are labelled. Reviewer assertions about Indian law are treated the same way — they are **[VERIFY]** questions the design must accommodate cheaply, not facts this document may state. Where a reviewer's fix was **not** taken, the refusal and its reason are written down (§11, closing note) rather than left silent.

**How Phase 7 evidence is labelled here, because that report is degraded and says so.** Nine of its twelve research passes terminated on search-budget exhaustion; its own fact-check graded eight passes *partly-inflated* and two *unreliable*, and it struck thirteen claims as fabricated or untraceable. Its self-assessed overall confidence is **LOW-MEDIUM**. Every claim imported from it below therefore carries the strength label it holds there — **[PHASE7 VERIFIED]** (confirmed against a live page during that report's own fact-check), **[PHASE7 SINGLE-SOURCE]**, **[PHASE7 UNVERIFIED]**, and **[PHASE7 UNEVIDENCED]** (the report searched for evidence on this point and found *none in either direction* — an absence of data, not a weak datum, and the strongest warning label in this document) — and none of them is promoted past **[VERIFY]**: a secondary tax blog is corroboration, not a CA sign-off, and §5's standing caveat governs every statutory figure it contributed. **The struck claims may not re-enter this document, a pitch deck or a financial model** — in particular *no market-share percentage for Tally*, *no e-invoice ₹2 Cr threshold or October-2025 date*, *no notice-incidence statistic*, and *no Udyam distribution figures*. (The "no ERPNext implementation figure above ₹5 L" rule is **lifted in revision 5** — two further partners published, and §8.4 answer 10 now carries the widened, sourced range.) Where the research had no evidence at all, this document says so rather than inheriting silence as comfort.

**How Phase 8 evidence is labelled, because it is a materially better base and must not be flattened into the Phase 7 discount.** `PHASE8-MARKET-RESEARCH-DEEP.md` re-ran the same ground with working India-region search: five of its eight passes were graded *trustworthy*, three *partly-inflated*, **none unreliable**, and its statutory section (§8) was graded **trustworthy with high confidence — 13 of 14 cited URLs fetched directly, every quote confirmed word-for-word on its `.gov.in` page**. Labels used below: **[PHASE8 PRIMARY]** — read on an official `.gov.in`, e-gazette or reported-judgment source and re-confirmed by that report's own audit; **[PHASE8 VERIFIED]** — read on the named vendor or forum page and re-confirmed by audit; **[PHASE8 SINGLE-SOURCE]**; **[PHASE8 UNEVIDENCED]** — searched for across two passes and ~250 queries, found in neither direction. **[PHASE8 PRIMARY] is the only label in this document that outranks [VERIFY] as a statement of what the rule *is*** — and it still does not outrank §5's standing caveat, which is about who signs for it. Two further rules inherited from that report and enforced here: **its §0.2 struck claims may never enter this document** (in particular the Terra Insight ₹15,147 / ₹8–12 lakh exposure model, which is a vendor's own hypothetical and must never enter a financial model; onebook.app's "94% of 50 CAs"; and three more uncited Tally market-share percentages), and **every Reddit quote it gathered is a search-engine-cached snippet nobody could re-read** — those may inform an interview guide and may not be cited, decked, or counted as a finding. Nothing tagged `[REDDIT-CACHE]` there appears anywhere here.

---

## 1. THE DECISION

Build a commercial, closed-source ERP for **Indian discrete and light-process manufacturers** — fabrication, machining, castings/forging, plastics, auto components — at ₹5–50 Cr turnover, 20–150 employees, 1–3 GSTINs, job-work-heavy, today running Tally + Excel + a payroll consultant + a CA.

Architecture: **TypeScript/NestJS on PostgreSQL 17 with Drizzle**, conventional typed models, and a **thin metadata overlay** (extension fields, property overrides, generic CRUD/list) generated *from* the typed schema — not a metadata engine (PHASE5 §1; PHASE2 §4 rejects the engine and is right about the engine).

v1 is **a GST-correct manufacturing ledger with a factory gate, a job-work challan register, a wage sheet, and a migration path off Tally — and nothing else.** Concretely: docstatus lifecycle with `document_date` separate from `posting_date` and statutory artifacts in side tables, chained audit trail, gapless (GSTIN, doctype, series, Indian FY) numbering with a mid-year cut-over path, effective-dated statutory rule store, GL + an append-only party subledger + a four-head tax engine, e-invoice and full e-way-bill lifecycle with a posted-vs-issuable gate and a defined failed-IRN path, GSTR-1/3B preparation **including the outward amendment tables and Documents Issued**, 2B reconciliation, stock with moving-average valuation keyed `(item, location)` under an explicit lock protocol, PO/GRN/SO/DC, gate entry and weighbridge, BOM/work order/job card, **Section 143 job-work register with challan chains, ageing against a window that models the Commissioner's extension, ITC-04 Tables 4/5A/5B/5C *and* the deemed-supply posting path with its interest computation**, PF/ESI/PT computation, and a Tally + opening-balance importer that carries open items at invoice level, opening challans, the series cut-over **and the statutory YTD registers the first year's returns depend on**.

Timeline: **not yet derivable — see §7.6.** The working figure of 9–11 months at 6–8 engineers is an extrapolation of a prior that its own author marked unmeasured, applied to a scope roughly 2× the entity count it was derived for. It must be re-derived bottom-up before it enters a funding decision. GA lands **2–3 months after engineering-complete**, because the gate is two pilots through a real month-end.

**Stage 0 is a hard go/no-go gate, not a warm-up.** Q1, Q2, Q3, Q7, Q8, Q15, Q17, Q19 and Q20 (§10) are answered by two people in three to four weeks with no engineers, and they decide whether this project should exist at all (§8.5). Q18 — who owns the clean-room protocol and what counsel costs — starts in the same window, because it is the only risk in §9.2 with no engineering remedy after it lands. Stage 1 does not start — and nobody is hired — until the go/no-go and a one-page financial model are written down and signed.

---

## 2. WHY THESE REFERENCES PRODUCED THIS PLAN

**Licensing position, fixed:** ERPNext, hrms, india-compliance and Odoo are GPL/AGPL/LGPL — **clean-room only: design and business rules, never code.** The Frappe *framework* repo is MIT and specific files are literally copyable **with attribution** (PHASE5 §9 Q8) — this requires a file-by-file provenance review confirming no GPL ERPNext code leaked into the framework repo, plus a maintained `ATTRIBUTIONS.md`. Everything we take from the GPL repos we pay for by rewriting.

**ERPNext (PHASE2).** The single largest contribution: ~110 business rules that are fifteen years of production defects converted into invariants — one GL posting choke point with a fixed ten-stage pipeline, COGS as the delta in stock value never `rate × qty`, outstanding derived by recomputation over an append-only signed subledger, cancel-as-contra. We take the *shapes*. It disqualified itself from the architecture question: PHASE2 §4 recommends against the metadata document engine, and its own §5/§6 catalogue — mutating submitted documents during repost, ~30 Singles toggles that change posting math with no rule stamp on the ledger row, status denormalised and written by `db_set` from dozens of call sites, hard-coded magic tolerances (`0.009`, flat `0.5`, `0.1`) outside the precision system — is a list of things to avoid, not adopt.

**Odoo (PHASE3).** Contributed ideas we adopt outright: **inventory as double-entry between locations** (Bet 3, "the highest-value single idea in the whole codebase"), the `GROUP BY voucher HAVING SUM(balance) != 0` balance assertion (Bet 2), **±100% repartition** as the reverse-charge and ineligible-ITC mechanism (cleaner than a boolean flag — PHASE4 §2.5 agrees), **interval algebra** as the one calendar primitive for shifts, leave and workcenter capacity, the **tri-quantity order ledger** (ordered/fulfilled/invoiced with status as a pure function), and **lead-time-horizon reorder forecasting**. It disqualified itself on: `safe_eval` stringly-typed domains, runtime DDL for analytic plans, recompute-on-read valuation (v19 deleted `stock.valuation.layer`; PHASE3 §6.17 and §7 Q1 call this a cautionary tale and recommend the pre-19 append-only layer), and the god-model packaging of `account_move.py` at 7,456 lines. Its 224 `l10n_*` packs are "20 years of accountant-hours" we cannot copy and must buy from a CA.

**Frappe framework (PHASE5).** Settled the architecture question — ADAPT, thin overlay over typed models, with the boundary criterion we now use everywhere. Contributed the 91-property allowlist and `allow_property_change` guards (MIT, copyable), the background-jobs and rate-limiter designs, and the Redis client-side-invalidation design. It disqualified itself as a platform: 18–30 engineer-months for framework parity **before the first invoice screen exists** (§1), ~99,000 LOC of generic Desk UI whose ceiling is categorical (zero paste handlers in the grid; `check_if_large_table` exists purely to disable features past 100k rows — §7.1), `ignore_permissions` appearing 775 times with the query engine's own default being the unsafe one (§7.7), and a `Version` audit table with eight verified gaps, six of them graded Fatal, against the Rule 11(g) requirement (§5.2). **It ships zero statutory content — grep-verified (§1.8).**

**india-compliance + hrms + frappe-books + the Tally question (PHASE4, PHASE6).** PHASE4 is the statutory gate: 14 unconditional capabilities without which the product is not sellable, and — critically — an evidence-labelling discipline that separates [CODE] from [VERIFY] from **[NOT IN CODE]**. The [NOT IN CODE] set is the product: Section 143 job-work deadlines (repo-wide grep returns only HSN digit coincidences), PF/ESI/PT rates (the string "ESI" appears in hrms only in a test fixture and a comment), turnover-based applicability, and effective-dated rules. PHASE6 added: no Tally integration exists in any of the five repos; no trial-balance CSV import exists anywhere in ERPNext; gate entry, weighbridge, delivery-challan-as-movement and shift-wise production rollup return zero grep hits across both erpnext and odoo/addons. frappe-books (MIT, TypeScript) proved a correct double-entry core is ~200 lines of typed code and gave us the `Transactional` lifecycle contract shape (PHASE6 §2) — but its `NumberSeries` is read→check→write with no lock and is explicitly not copied.

**PHASE1 (ever-gauzy, IDURAR)** contributed nothing to the domain and is not cited below. Neither is an ERP: no journal, no chart of accounts, no GL posting, invoice tax computed in the Angular client with the server persisting whatever the browser posts; inventory is a two-table quantity cache with no moves, lots or valuation. Gauzy's plugin decorator and its tenancy base class are the only ideas worth a paragraph, and both are superseded by PHASE5's overlay design.

---

## 3. ARCHITECTURE

### 3.1 Stack

**TypeScript (strict) on Bun · NestJS 11 · PostgreSQL 17 · Drizzle ORM · Next.js 16 / React 19 · BullMQ + Redis.**

This mirrors the team's existing stack. There is no argument for Python: the only thing it would buy is code-level reuse from ERPNext/Odoo, which is GPL and therefore forbidden.

```
apps/     api (NestJS) · web (Next) · worker (BullMQ) · migrator (CLI)
packages/ domain · ledger · registry · db · money · print · ui · env
```

**ORM — Drizzle, not Prisma.** The one deviation from the CRM's stack, decided by five hard requirements:

| Requirement | Prisma 7 | Drizzle |
|---|---|---|
| `SELECT … FOR UPDATE NOWAIT` on the series counter (PHASE6 §1.1) | raw only | first-class |
| Recursive CTEs (BOM explosion, account tree, PLE outstanding — PHASE2 §3.1) | raw only | supported |
| CHECK constraints, partial indexes, partitions, `REVOKE UPDATE/DELETE` on audit | outside the model | same migration file |
| Addressing a JSONB key the generated client never saw (extension overlay) | impossible | `sql` fragments compose |
| `numeric` never becoming a JS `number` | Decimal.js object | `string` → our `Money` |

Migrations: `drizzle-kit generate` emits plain `.sql` we hand-edit and commit — PHASE5 §8.1's declarative-diff requirement, with down-migrations hand-written for anything destructive.

**PostgreSQL only.** Unanimous across PHASE2 Q3, PHASE3 Q4, PHASE5 §8.1. It deletes an entire bug class: ERPNext carries MariaDB-gap-lock vs Postgres-advisory-lock branches in at least three places and an Item Price `ORDER BY name` tiebreak added *only* because the two engines picked different rows; PHASE5 documents a multi-DB SQL-rewriting regex whose in-tree comment records a real past data corruption.

**Money is a type, not a number.** Storage: money `numeric(18,4)`, quantity `numeric(18,6)`, unit price `numeric(18,6)`, tax rate `numeric(9,6)`, FX rate `numeric(18,9)`. We reject Frappe's uniform `decimal(21,9)` — PHASE5 §2.3 correctly notes it makes a Percent indistinguishable from a Currency. In TypeScript no money value is ever a `number`. `packages/money` exports **three** types, and the distinction between them is load-bearing:

| Type | Role |
|---|---|
| `Money` (bigint minor units at scale 1e4, **plus a currency tag**), `Qty` (bigint, scale 1e6) | **Persisted** scales. Constructed only at the SQL boundary from the `string` Drizzle returns, and only ever produced by an explicit `commit()`. **`Money` carries its currency in the type** — `add(Money<INR>, Money<USD>)` does not type-check. Revision 2's table described a bare branded bigint while **D5** said "a `Money` type carrying currency"; the type is the one that wins, because the four-leg GL and the realised-FX rule both depend on it. |
| `Dec` | **Arbitrary-precision intermediate**, working precision 12 significant decimals. *Every* arithmetic operator returns `Dec` — `mul`, `div`, `add`, `sub`. `Money × Qty` is a `Dec`, not a `Money`. Backed by `BigInt`, never a JS `number`: `rate × qty × tax` on a large invoice line overflows a 53-bit float long before it overflows the persisted scale, and a silent precision loss there is indistinguishable from a correct number. |
| `commit(dec, scale) → Money \| Qty` | The **only** rounding boundary. Rounding mode is **half-away-from-zero — [VERIFY] q85**, stated in revision 1 and 2 as "the Indian statutory convention" with no citation anywhere in the scout. It is the convention both references implement, which is evidence of practice, not of statute; the mode is therefore a rule-store row read once at bootstrap, not a constant, so a CA's answer costs a data change. Division rounds at the working precision, then `commit` rounds once more at the target scale. |

This exists because T3(a) requires the unrounded value to survive into the e-invoice payload, which a fixed 4-dp type cannot hold, and because T2's inclusive back-solve divides by `(1 + Σslope)` — a non-terminating decimal on the first line of the tax engine. `Dec` is where that lives. One `round` path, and a lint rule banning `Math.round` and bare `/` on branded types in `packages/domain` and `packages/ledger`. **`packages/money` is written first**; it is a two-day job every other package imports. **Every tolerance derives from the precision system** — a literal numeric tolerance in money code is a lint failure. This is the direct fix for PHASE2 §5's catalogue of magic tolerances and for the float-dust symptom PHASE2 §3.4 records (`round_off_if_near_zero(precision=7)`, "without it FIFO queues accumulate 1e-13 ghost bins forever").

**Two policies that are cheap now and archaeology later.** *UoM conversion precision:* conversion factors are `numeric(18,9)`; a converted quantity is a `Dec` and is committed exactly once at `Qty` scale, except where a rule names a direction — M9 ceils for whole-number UoMs, P1 applies `ROUND_CEILING` in the purchase UoM, V12 down-rounds to the move UoM then half-up back. Those three are the only sanctioned deviations and each is named in its rule. *Timezone:* every `timestamptz` is stored in UTC and every **business date** (`posting_date`, `document_date`, `supply_date`, attendance date, EWB validity boundary) is derived in the **tenant's** timezone, which is a NOT NULL column on the tenant record. Biometric ingestion, the 24-hour IRN window, EWB midnight-based validity and M7's "batch by timezone" all read it. A product that computes an attendance date in UTC marks a 06:00 IST punch-in as the previous day, which is a wage error. Recorded as part of **D17** in §9.1.

### 3.2 The metadata / typed-code boundary

**Criterion (PHASE5 §1):** metadata where cost scales with *entity count* and behaviour is uniform; typed code where cost scales with *business complexity* and the behaviour is **money**.

**Typed code — the only source of truth:**
- Both ledgers (`gl_entry`, `stock_ledger_entry`) and their posting services.
- **Submittable documents — the real list, 27.** Sales Invoice, Purchase Invoice, Credit/Debit Note, **Bill of Supply**, **Receipt Voucher**, **Payment Voucher**, **Refund Voucher**, Delivery Note, Delivery Challan, Purchase Receipt, Payment Entry, Journal Entry, Stock Entry, Stock Reconciliation, Landed Cost Voucher, Material Request, SO, PO, Work Order, Job Card, BOM, Quality Inspection, Period Closing Voucher, **Subcontracting Challan (out)**, **Subcontracting Receipt**, **Bill of Entry**, **Inter-GSTIN Branch Transfer Invoice**.
  The four voucher types and Bill of Supply were missing from revision 1 and are not optional for a monthly filer: an exempt or nil-rated outward supply (scrap, exempted goods) must not be issued on a *tax invoice* series, and an RCM purchase from an unregistered supplier needs the payment-voucher half as well as the self-invoice half. Each carries its own series and a `series_nature`, which is what GSTR-1 Table 13 reports on (§3.6). **[VERIFY] q71** — the exact document set and the Rule 46/49/55 particulars each must print.
- Masters: Item, UOM+UQC + conversion factors, **Batch**, Warehouse/Location, Account, Cost Center, **Bank Account**, **Party as a supertype table with real FKs** (no Dynamic Link polymorphism), **Address**, **Contact**, **Transporter + Vehicle**, **Price List / Item Price**, Tax Template + Item Tax Template + Tax Category, Company, GSTIN Registration, **Authorised Signatory (per GSTIN)**, Fiscal Year, **Workstation, Operation, Employee, Salary Structure, Shift Type, Shift Assignment**. Batch is listed because V14 freezes `has_batch_no` against a table revision 2 never declared; Authorised Signatory because Rule 46(q)/Rule 55(1) print a signature and the IRP-signed QR substitutes only where e-invoicing applies (**[VERIFY] q86**).
- Operational entities: **Gate Entry**, **Weighbridge Ticket**, **Salary Slip**, **Attendance**, **ITC-04 log**, **e-Way Bill artifact**, **e-Invoice artifact**, **GSTR log**, **statutory rule store** (§3.9).
- **Count, honestly: ~40 top-level entities, not ~18 — and that is still not the number that sizes the build.** Revision 1's figure omitted Address (which T4 resolves supplier state from and T14 blocks post-submit edits to), Transporter (EWB Part A needs its ID/GSTIN), the whole payroll set, and every entity §3.5 itself names in mandatory audit coverage. The second review round put the real figure at **55–70 once child tables are counted as entities** (document lines, tax lines, repartition rows, BOM items, job-card time logs, salary-slip components, challan lines, artifact rows), and it is right that those carry schema, validation, audit and migration cost even though they have no screen. **This document does not pick between 40 and 70; the enumerated entity register is a Stage 0 deliverable (§7) and it, not a round number, feeds the re-derivation in §7.6.** Every duration estimate in §7 is downstream of that register, which is why §7.6 refuses to restate the old one.
- **All money logic**: costing, valuation, GST computation, place-of-supply, IRN payload, e-way bill, TDS/TCS, payroll.
- Core screens hand-built in React. Schema migrations. Reports as on-disk TS modules — contract in §3.10. (PHASE3 §5 notes Odoo Community ships financial report *definitions* with no evaluator at all, so "just use Odoo" fails here too; a declarative report engine pays off at 60 localisations, and we ship one country.)
- **Screen count, honestly.** Revision 1 said "~40 hand-built screens" and that number excluded the GSTR workbench, the 2B reconciliation UI, the import wizard, the Tally review-and-map UI, the gate kiosk, the shop-floor job-card timer, the §143 register, the amendment workbench and ~25 report filter screens. The second review round put it at **70–90**. The enumerated list is a Stage 0 deliverable and a precondition of any duration estimate (§7.6), and the **UI workstream that builds them is named with its own headcount in §3.13** — revision 2 costed screens and staffed nobody to design them.

**Metadata — derived, not authored:**
- **Field registry**, generated at *build time* from the Drizzle schema into `registry.json`. Metadata is a projection of code, not a substitute for it. This inversion satisfies PHASE2's actual objection (no static types, rules as eval'd strings, schema mutation as a supported operation) while keeping PHASE5's commercial payoff.
- **Extension fields** (`field_extension`) and **property overrides** (`property_override`).
- Generic REST/RPC CRUD + list/filter/sort/export over the registry.
- Declarative permissions compiled to typed predicates; audit writer that walks the row; print formats (Nunjucks rows) → HTML → PDF via Chromium/Playwright (wkhtmltopdf is abandoned; PHASE5 §8.2 #14); webhooks.
- **The Nunjucks exception to "no code execution, ever", stated rather than left as a contradiction.** D12 forbids customer-authored code, and a tenant-editable server-rendered template *is* customer-authored execution with a template-injection surface. It stays in v1 because "nobody lays out their own GST invoice" is only true until the first customer wants their logo in a different place — but it runs under a stated confinement: autoescape on; **no `{% include %}`, no `{% import %}`, no `{% extends %}`**; a fixed filter allowlist; a data context that is a plain serialised view model with no ORM handles, no `ctx`, no connection; rendering in the **worker** process, never the API, under a CPU and wall-clock budget; and the template body audited like any other row. If any of that proves unenforceable, the fallback is developer-authored formats on disk that tenants may pick and skin but not edit — which is the honest position and costs a support ticket, not a CVE.
- A typed JSON-AST expression DSL for `depends_on` / `mandatory_depends_on` / `fetch_from` only, one compiled evaluator shared by browser and server. **No string eval, no sandbox, no server scripts, ever** (PHASE5 §7.7; PHASE4 Q14 — `eval` in multi-tenant SaaS is remote code execution).

**Extension fields are JSONB, not runtime ALTER TABLE. This overrides PHASE5 §8.1 item 6.** PHASE5 recommends real additive ALTER via the migration process *and* declarative-diff migrations, and does not notice these collide: a desired-state differ computes DROP for any column not in the model, so tenant #47's extra column must be hand-audited on every future diff forever. Frappe escapes this only because its differ is additive-only and hand-rolled — the thing PHASE5 tells us to discard. **Decision:** every extensible entity carries `ext jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'`; extension fields are keys registered in `field_extension` with type, label, validation and provenance. Consequences: adding a field is an INSERT not DDL (no lock, no migration window, no orphan-column reaper — PHASE5 §4.5's problem deleted); the base schema stays 100% tool-owned; the audit writer serialises the whole row including `ext`, so customer fields are audited the instant they exist with no registry walk. **Promotion path:** when an extension field needs an index, a unique constraint or a hot report filter, the migrator adds `col GENERATED ALWAYS AS ((ext->>'key')::type) STORED` plus its index — a normal reviewed migration, tenant data untouched. Cost accepted honestly: no per-column NOT NULL/FK for extension fields; validation is enforced by the registry-driven validator at the single write chokepoint. **Statutory fields are never extension fields**, so nothing a CA cares about depends on this.

`property_override` is keyed **`(entity, field, property, layer)`** where `layer ∈ {core, tenant}` in v1. **`vertical_app` is deleted from the enum until the module contract exists (§3.11).** Revision 1 shipped a three-valued key whose middle value had no referent anywhere in the document — worse than either choice. It is the dimension Frappe lacks, whose absence means an app-shipped setter silently destroys a customer's change with no conflict, no warning and no log entry (PHASE5 §4.3(i)). Permission overlays **merge, never replace** (§4.3(ii) — Frappe's source carries `# TODO/XXX: Docperm have no "sync" as of now. They get OVERRIDDEN on sync`, which for statutory roles is a live exposure). The **"explain this field" inspector ships in the same PR as the overlay** (§4.3(iii)) — retrofitting observability onto a multi-table merge is what makes these systems undebuggable at year three.

**The merge itself, which revision 2 never specified.** Three sources produce the object the API, the validator, the audit writer, generic CRUD and the inspector all read: the **build-time `registry.json`** generated from the Drizzle schema, the tenant's **`field_extension`** rows, and the layered **`property_override`** rows. Resolution order is `base registry → field_extension (adds fields) → property_override by layer precedence (core, then tenant)`, producing one immutable merged descriptor per entity per tenant. It is **cached per tenant schema, keyed on `(tenant, registry_build_id, overlay_version)`**, where `overlay_version` is a counter bumped by any write to either overlay table; the bump is the invalidation event and it is published on the same Redis client-side-invalidation channel as everything else (§3.7). No wall-clock TTL — a stale form that silently ignores a mandatory flag is a compliance defect, not a cache miss. The inspector reads the same merged descriptor plus its provenance chain, which is what makes a validation error able to name *which* layer made a field mandatory.

**The field-render contract — what makes the overlay mean anything on hand-built React.** `property_override` sells relabel / hide / make-mandatory / change-precision on *core* fields, but a hand-written JSX form ignores all of it unless every field reads the registry. The contract: **one `<Field name="sales_invoice.delivery_date">` component** resolves label, visibility, mandatory-ness and precision from the compiled registry at render, and every hand-built screen composes those. Layout stays hand-written; properties stay registry-driven; the two are orthogonal. Extension fields render in a declared `ext` slot per section, and the layered merge surfaces its provenance in the inspector so a validation error names *which* layer made the field mandatory. One worked example (Sales Invoice) ships with the component, or the abstraction is untested.

**Sequencing, corrected.** Only `ext jsonb` + `field_extension` are genuinely day-one — they are a column, and columns are what you cannot retrofit. `property_override`, the 91-property allowlist and the inspector **move out of Stage 1 into a Stage 2 slot gated on Q9**, because §10 Q9 calls extension-field demand "the single highest-leverage piece of missing evidence in this entire scout" and says answer it before the overlay is built — which revision 1 then contradicted by building it in months 0–2.

The 91-property allowlist and `allow_property_change` guards are copied verbatim from Frappe (MIT, `customize_form.py`), copyright preserved. PHASE5 §4.4 is also honest about the ceiling: real customer requests routinely fall outside the allowlist and then need a fork anyway. Build it; never sell on it.

### 3.3 Document lifecycle

Every document table carries the spine:

```sql
id            uuid primary key,        -- UUIDv7, client-generatable → idempotent submit
doc_no        text,                    -- NULL until submit (§3.6)
series_id     uuid references document_series(id),   -- chosen at entry, never inferred (§3.6)
docstatus     smallint not null default 0 check (docstatus in (0,1,2)),
document_date date not null,           -- the date the document BEARS
posting_date  date not null,           -- the date it hits the ledger
gst_report_period text,                -- the return period it is reported in (§3.4)
check (document_date <= posting_date),
amended_from  uuid references <self>(id),
amend_seq     smallint not null default 0,
company_id    uuid not null, registration_id uuid not null,   -- NOT NULL scoping
created_at timestamptz, created_by uuid, updated_at timestamptz, updated_by uuid
```

Legal transitions, enforced in one place:

```
draft --submit--> submitted --cancel--> cancelled --amend--> (new draft, amended_from=<id>)
draft --delete--> gone (audit records the deletion with a full snapshot)
```

There is no other transition. `submitted → draft` does not exist.

**`document_date` is not `posting_date`, and revision 2 had only one of them.** The scout's own evidence forces the split: the IRN payload carries a document date; EWB validity runs from generation; GST returns are keyed on the document's date while the ledger is ordered by `(posting_date, seq)`; and the §143 deemed-supply invoice (§3.4) exists precisely because those two dates diverge. Invariants, stated once: `document_date ≤ posting_date`; the GSTR-1/3B builders read `gst_report_period`, never `posting_date`; ageing, interest and statutory windows read `document_date` or an explicit `supply_date`, never `posting_date`; and `posting_date` alone is what the four period gates test. A document whose `document_date` falls in a period already marked filed is refused by the same gate that refuses a back-dated posting. Adding this after posted rows exist is a ledger backfill plus a re-run of every return — **D17** in §9.1.

**Immutability.** On `docstatus = 1` every column is immutable except a per-entity `allowOnSubmit` allowlist. For statutory documents that allowlist contains only framework-written derived status fields (`status`, `per_billed`, `per_received`, `outstanding_amount`), written exclusively by derivation services — never by a user, never by ad-hoc `db_set`. **Enforcement is a database trigger**, not a service convention: `BEFORE UPDATE` raises unless the changed column set is a subset of the allowlist and `docstatus` is unchanged or transitioning 1→2.

**Government responses live in side tables, not on the document.** Revision 1 carried IRN, ack no/date, signed QR and EWB number as `no_copy` columns on the document while §3.8 made the government call asynchronous — so a background job would have had to UPDATE a submitted statutory document, which the trigger above rejects unconditionally. The design was self-defeating. Corrected:

```sql
create table einvoice_artifact (
  id uuid primary key, document_type text not null, document_id uuid not null,
  attempt_seq int not null,                 -- append-only; retries add rows
  status text not null,                     -- pending|generated|failed|cancelled
  irn text, ack_no text, ack_date timestamptz, signed_invoice text, signed_qr text,
  irp_response jsonb, error_code text, is_sandbox boolean not null,
  ruleset_version_id uuid, created_at timestamptz not null, created_by uuid not null,
  unique (document_type, document_id, attempt_seq)
);
-- ewb_artifact(document_id, attempt_seq, ewb_no, valid_upto, part_b_seq,
--   vehicle_no, transporter_id, status, …) with the same append-only lifecycle.
```

The document holds only an FK-free derived `irn_status` maintained by the same derivation-service mechanism as `status`, and the artifact tables are the record. Part-B vehicle updates and transporter reassignment append rows; nothing is ever overwritten. This also gives EWB the append-only lifecycle its Part-A/Part-B/extension sequence actually has.

**Posted is not issued — the Rule 48(5) gate. [VERIFY] q72.** R1 ("never block document submission on a government call") is right about *posting* and was wrong about *issuing*. For a taxpayer above the e-invoicing threshold, a document issued other than through the IRP is understood not to be a valid invoice, so the goods cannot move on it and the recipient's ITC is exposed. Therefore: **print, email, dispatch and gate-out are blocked while `irn_status ∈ {pending, failed}`** on an e-invoice-applicable document, with a visible "awaiting IRN" state on the dispatch screen and a supervisor-only offline-fallback path that records a reason. IRN and the IRP-signed QR are mandatory elements of the tax-invoice print contract. The gate refuses a consignment whose invoice has no IRN — revision 1 had nothing stopping a driver leaving with an unIRN'd invoice. A service-layer guard is not sufficient for a compliance argument.

**The permanently-failed IRN, which revision 2 had no path for.** D6's error triage has a class 1 — permanently invalid, do not retry — and revision 2 left that state with a consumed number, a posted invoice, no IRN, and a GSTR-1 that would report it. Defined path, and it is a tested workflow rather than a support ticket: the document is **cancelled and amended inside the IRP's cancellation window**, the failed number is retained and disclosed as cancelled in Table 13 (§3.6), the amended document acquires a fresh number and a fresh IRN attempt, and the `einvoice_artifact` row keeps the IRP's rejection payload as the evidence of why. If the window has already closed, the remedy is a s.34 credit note and a fresh invoice, never a silent edit. **[VERIFY] q89** — whether the IRP's own cancellation window, or the s.34 route, is the correct remedy in each case; the mechanism is the same either way and only the branch condition is a rule-store row.

**The print contract, which neither revision wrote down.** A CA fails an invoice on sight for things no schema catches. In v1 the print contract therefore carries, **all [VERIFY] q86**: an **authorised-signatory reference per GSTIN** with a signature image or DSC reference (Rule 46(q) / Rule 55(1) — the IRP-signed QR substitutes only where e-invoicing applies, so every B2C invoice, bill of supply and delivery challan still needs one); **copy-type as a print-time parameter** (Original for Recipient / Duplicate for Transporter / Triplicate for Supplier on goods invoices, and the Rule 55(2) equivalents on challans); an **invoice-cum-bill-of-supply variant** for a mixed taxable/exempt supply to an unregistered person (Rule 46A), which is a document variant and a series decision, not a layout tweak; and the **SKD/CKD and batch-consignment chain** (Rule 55(4)) — complete invoice with the first consignment, a challan per subsequent consignment, the original invoice referenced on each — which structural fabricators and machinery builders use routinely and which is a real document linkage, not a print option. None of these is expensive; all of them are a re-model if the document types and series are wrong. Repost/revaluation may **never** rewrite `basic_rate`/`valuation_rate` on submitted child rows — ERPNext does, and PHASE2 §5 flags it as defeating the immutability the ledger design otherwise buys.

**Cancel = reversal, always. Immutable-ledger mode is the only mode.** `cancel()` appends a mirrored reversing set: swapped debit/credit (or negated qty), `reverses_entry_id` FK to the original, `voucher_id` unchanged, `posting_date = today`. "Is this reversed?" is **derived** (`EXISTS … WHERE reverses_entry_id = e.id`), never a stored flag set by UPDATE — because UPDATE on posted rows is forbidden. frappe-books gets this 90% right (`revert()` writes a mirrored row) and 10% wrong (it also flags the original `reverted = true`); we take the 90%. One behaviour, no flag: PHASE2 §5 lists two-implementations-behind-a-flag as an anti-pattern and PHASE4 §7 notes Odoo force-enables exactly this for Indian companies. Cancellation is blocked if the period is closed, a downstream document consumes it, or an IRN exists past its 24h window.

**Amendment.** Clones a cancelled document into a new draft, `amend_seq+1`, `doc_no = NULL`; no statutory artifact is cloned, because artifacts are keyed to the original document id and never copy. On submit it gets a **new** number; the cancelled number is never reused and never returned. `amend_seq > 3` requires a supervisor role. The new document carries `original_doc_no`, `original_doc_date` and `amendment_period` so it can be reported in a GSTR-1 amendment table (§3.3a).

*Evidence label corrected:* revision 1 said "GSTR-1 requires cancelled numbers disclosed in-sequence." What PHASE6 §1.1 and PHASE4 §9.2 actually say is that the Documents Issued table reports issued/cancelled/net per series and that **gaps are an audit finding** — a reporting shape and an audit consequence, not a cited statutory prohibition. Rule 46(b) requires a consecutive series, which is not the same claim. The design is unchanged (keep cancelled numbers, disclose them); the *justification* is downgraded, and §3.6's blocking behaviour is softened accordingly.

### 3.3a Correcting a filed period

Revision 1 had no lawful route to correct anything after a return was filed, and its `cancel()` silently rewrote filed periods: the reversal posts at `posting_date = today` so the GSTR-1-filed gate never fires, but the original drops to `docstatus 2` and GSTR-1 is built from `docstatus 1` only (PHASE4 §4.1 universal exclusions) — so regenerating September's return in November no longer matches what was filed. Three corrections:

1. **Cancellation is blocked once the document's `(GSTIN, return_period)` GSTR-1 or 3B log is marked filed** (§3.4 gate (d)). The remedy is a credit/debit note under s.34 or an amendment row — never a cancel. **[VERIFY] q73:** the s.34(2) deadline for credit notes (understood to be 30 November following the FY end) becomes a rule-store row that blocks a late credit note.
2. **The six outward amendment tables ship in v1** — B2BA, B2CLA, CDNRA, CDNURA, EXPA, B2CSA. PHASE4 §4.3 covers the *inward* amended categories only; the outward set is absent from PHASE4 and revision 1 never noticed. Every real customer amends something inside 90 days of go-live, so revision 1's GA gate would have passed in month one and failed in month two.
3. **`is_latest_data` invalidation** (PHASE4 §4.5): any write touching a period whose return log exists clears the flag on that log, and the GSTR workbench shows the divergence rather than silently regenerating.

**The lifecycle contract** — adopted at design level from frappe-books' `Transactional.ts` (PHASE6 §2: "a genuinely better interface than scattering hooks across controllers"):

```ts
interface Submittable<T> {
  validate(doc: T, ctx: Ctx): Promise<void>;
  getPosting(doc: T, ctx: Ctx): Promise<Posting | null>;   // the ONLY money hook
  onSubmitSideEffects?(doc: T, ctx: Ctx): Promise<void>;   // status propagation only
}
```

The framework wires validate → post → number → audit → side-effects → commit. No ladder of 15 hooks (PHASE5 §2.5), no per-hook webhook/script lookup on the hot path.

**Dry-run preview.** Compute the GL and SLE the document will produce, show them, roll back (PHASE2 §3.4 / §5: "the single best UX idea in the codebase"). Cheap, and the highest-trust thing we can show a CA in a demo.

### 3.4 Ledger core

Two append-only ledgers, one posting chokepoint each. Both are INSERT-only; the application role has no UPDATE/DELETE grant on either.

**`gl_entry`** — one row per account leg. From Odoo Bet 2 we keep "one ledger table for every financial document"; we reject the god-model packaging — the accounting facet lives in `gl_entry`, the commercial facet (item, qty, rate, discount) on the document's line table, joined by `voucher_line_id`. Odoo's 7,456-line `account_move.py` is what happens if you merge them.

```sql
create table gl_entry (
  id uuid primary key,
  seq bigint generated always as identity unique,   -- THE total order
  posting_date date not null,                        -- ledger date; the four period gates test THIS
  document_date date not null,                       -- the date the source document bears (§3.3)
  gst_report_period text,                            -- the return period, stamped at post time
  voucher_type text not null, voucher_id uuid not null, voucher_line_id uuid,
  account_id uuid not null references account(id),
  party_id uuid references party(id),
  cost_center_id uuid not null references cost_center(id),   -- NOT NULL: §4.1B, D10
  debit numeric(18,4) not null default 0, credit numeric(18,4) not null default 0,
  debit_ac_ccy numeric(18,4) not null default 0,     -- four amount legs, stored,
  credit_ac_ccy numeric(18,4) not null default 0,    -- never reconverted at report time
  account_currency text not null, exchange_rate numeric(18,9) not null,
  company_id uuid not null, registration_id uuid not null, fiscal_year_id uuid not null,
  dimensions jsonb not null default '{}',            -- CLOSED key set, empty in v1; see below
  reverses_entry_id uuid references gl_entry(id),
  ruleset_version_id uuid not null,                  -- §3.9, not a single rule
  is_opening boolean not null default false,
  is_migration boolean not null default false,       -- L11; the only writer is the migrator
  created_at timestamptz not null default now(), created_by uuid not null,
  check (debit >= 0 and credit >= 0),
  check (debit * credit = 0)                         -- Odoo's CHECK, verbatim semantics
);
create index on gl_entry (account_id, posting_date, seq);
create index on gl_entry (voucher_type, voucher_id);
create unique index on gl_entry (reverses_entry_id) where reverses_entry_id is not null;
```

**Three DDL defects the second review round found, corrected above.** (i) `cost_center_id` was **nullable** while §4.1B, §3.8 and D10 all require it NOT NULL — the whole point of the NOT NULL scoping set is that a blank-means-allow footgun cannot exist, and one nullable column reintroduced it. Every company gets a default cost centre at setup so the constraint is satisfiable on day one. (ii) There was **no unique index on `reverses_entry_id`**, so a double-cancel race could write two reversals against one entry and the ledger would balance while the account was wrong; the partial unique index closes it. (iii) `dimensions jsonb` sat on the ledger while D11 says "one analytic axis, not N", with nothing stating what was legal in it — **the key set is closed and empty in v1**: the analytic axis is `cost_center_id`, the column exists only so that a second axis arrives as a registry-declared key plus a promoted generated column rather than a ledger migration, and only the migrator may register a key. `finance_book` is **removed from the merge key** in step 5 below: multi-book is deferred with fixed assets (D9), and a merge key naming a column that does not exist is a compile error waiting for the first developer.

**`seq` is the sole ordering key.** Ordering is always `(posting_date, seq)`, never `(posting_datetime, creation)` with tiebreak heuristics. PHASE2 §6's own verdict: "use a monotonic ledger sequence number and delete this entire bug class." Accounting dimensions are `jsonb` + promoted generated columns — never runtime-ALTERed columns on `gl_entry` and ~60 other tables, which PHASE2 §4 calls "the single most damaging pattern here" (Odoo's runtime `x_plan{id}_id` column creation is the same mistake — PHASE3 §6.8).

**`stock_ledger_entry`** — inventory as **double-entry between locations** (Odoo Bet 3). Nothing is ever incremented in isolation; every movement is a `move` with two **one-sided** SLE rows sharing a `move_id`. Receipts come from a `supplier`-usage virtual location, dispatches go to `customer`, scrap to `inventory-loss`, consumption to `production`, and **job work to a subcontractor location that is internal-usage and company-owned** — which makes PHASE4 §5.1's non-negotiable rule (goods at the job worker remain the principal's inventory) fall out of the location type instead of being a special case. Physical counts produce real moves, never an overwrite.

*This resolves a contradiction revision 1 shipped.* It described the SLE as one two-sided row `(from_location, to_location, qty)` while importing V6, V10, V11 and V1 verbatim from ERPNext's **one-sided, per-location** model — `qty_after_transaction`, `stock_value_difference` per row, `delta = qty_after − previous balance`. Four named rules assumed a shape the schema did not have. One-sided-with-`move_id` keeps Odoo's semantics (nothing is incremented in isolation; the move is the unit) and ERPNext's per-location arithmetic.

```sql
create table stock_ledger_entry (
  id uuid primary key,
  seq bigint generated always as identity unique,
  move_id uuid not null,                       -- pairs the two sides
  posting_date date not null,
  voucher_type text not null, voucher_id uuid not null, voucher_line_id uuid,
  item_id uuid not null, location_id uuid not null, batch_id uuid,
  actual_qty numeric(18,6) not null,           -- signed; +in / -out
  qty_after_transaction numeric(18,6) not null,-- per (item, location), from the locked balance
  valuation_rate numeric(18,6),                -- moving average AFTER this row
  stock_value numeric(18,4) not null,
  stock_value_difference numeric(18,4) not null, -- V1: this is COGS, never rate × qty
  owner_party_id uuid,                         -- V4: physically present, financially absent
  is_cancelled boolean not null default false,
  company_id uuid not null, registration_id uuid not null,
  ruleset_version_id uuid not null, created_at timestamptz not null, created_by uuid not null
);
create index on stock_ledger_entry (item_id, location_id, posting_date, seq);

create table stock_balance (                    -- posting-path authority, NOT a cache
  item_id uuid not null, location_id uuid not null,
  qty numeric(18,6) not null, value numeric(18,4) not null,
  valuation_rate numeric(18,6) not null, last_sle_seq bigint not null,
  primary key (item_id, location_id)
);
```

```sql
create table stock_valuation_layer (      -- the value record; authoritative, not a cache
  id uuid primary key,
  seq bigint generated always as identity unique,
  sle_id uuid not null references stock_ledger_entry(id),
  move_id uuid not null,
  item_id uuid not null, location_id uuid not null,
  qty_change numeric(18,6) not null, value_change numeric(18,4) not null,
  qty_after numeric(18,6) not null, value_after numeric(18,4) not null,
  avg_rate_after numeric(18,6) not null,
  prev_layer_id uuid references stock_valuation_layer(id),
  posting_date date not null, created_at timestamptz not null, created_by uuid not null
);
```

**The valuation key is `(item, location)`. Batch is a tracking dimension, not a valuation dimension. [D16]** Revision 2 declared `batch_id` on the SLE and never said whether the moving average was per batch, and the two readings are not compatible: S5's "expired batches value at zero" implies batch-level value, while a per-batch average multiplies the lock surface, the layer count and the repost cost by the batch cardinality of a fabrication shop. **Decision: one average per `(item, location)`.** Batch genealogy, expiry and FEFO removal are tracked on the SLE and on the batch master and drive *which* physical stock moves; they do not drive *what it is worth*. S5's expired-batch rule is therefore implemented as an explicit **write-down movement** to the inventory-loss location at the moment of expiry — a real move with a real GL effect that a CA can see — rather than as a silent valuation-time special case. This is as unretrofittable as the serialization contract: changing the valuation key after layers exist means recomputing every layer in the ledger, so it is **D16** in §9.1 and not a v1.1 decision.

**Serialization — the concurrency contract revision 1 omitted entirely.** Moving average is a read-modify-write against the tail of an append-only ledger: you must read prior qty and value to compute the new average. Two concurrent GRNs on the same `(item, location)` under READ COMMITTED both read the same prior state and both write a wrong average, silently and permanently — and V6's negative-stock check has the identical race. PHASE2 §6 records that the reference checkout carries **Postgres advisory-lock gates in `stock_ledger.py:306-331`** for exactly this; revision 1 cited that line only for "pick one database", taking the portability half of the finding and dropping the concurrency half.

> Contract: any transaction writing an SLE takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext(item_id || ':' || location_id))` for **every** affected pair, **acquired in sorted key order** across the whole document so two documents touching the same pairs cannot deadlock, **before** reading `stock_balance`. Posting transactions run at READ COMMITTED; the lock, not the isolation level, is the guarantee. The lock set is recorded on the posting trace. This is **D13** in §9.1 — as unretrofittable as numbering and strictly more damaging when wrong.

**The posting chokepoint.** One function creates every GL row. Fixed, load-bearing order (restated in our own terms from PHASE2 §3.1 `general_ledger.py:34`):

1. Resolve accounts, currencies, fiscal year, rates.
2. **Period gates, four, all before any math**: (a) accounting-period document lock with a named exempted role; (b) `accounts_frozen_till` hard date — **admin explicitly denied the bypass**; (c) Period Closing Voucher gate; (d) **GSTR-1-filed period lock** (PHASE4 §2.6 V12) — without it the books and the filed return diverge with no reconciliation path back.

   *Gate (d) needs a writer, which revision 1 did not have.* The reference learns filing status from the portal's Returns state machine (PHASE4 §4.5/§4.6: `filing_status`, ARN, `RETSTATUS` polling), all of which sits behind the OTP-authenticated Returns session we defer to v1.5 — so the gate read a field nothing wrote. Corrected: **"mark period filed" is an explicit, audited action** on the `gstr_log(gstin, return_type, period)` record, performed by a named role, requiring the **ARN and filing date** as attached evidence, with `reason` mandatory. Nothing else sets it. If the CA files and forgets, the period stays open and the divergence surfaces in the workbench rather than being invented by the system. A period may be un-marked only by a supervisor, with a reason, and the un-mark is itself audited.

   **One named exemption, keyed to a voucher type, not a role:** the `deemed_supply_143` voucher may pass gates (b) and (d) with a mandatory reason, because it is the one document whose supply date is statutorily prior. Everything else is refused. See the back-dating paragraph below for how it avoids back-dating at all.
3. Disabled/group-account rejection.
4. Cost-centre allocation explosion (percentages total exactly 100; no chaining).
5. Merge — lines collapse only when account, cost_center, party(+type), voucher_line_id, against_voucher(+type) **and every registered dimension key** match. Then drop zero rows. (`finance_book` was in revision 2's key and in no table; multi-book is deferred with D9.)
6. Sign normalisation — a negative debit becomes a positive credit. No negative amounts stored.
7. Balance check + round-off with a **precision-derived** tolerance (never ERPNext's flat `0.5`), round-off line on the company round-off account, **re-check after the insert**.
8. Minimum two surviving lines.
9. Party-subledger mirror into `payment_ledger_entry`.
10. Insert.

If a voucher class legitimately breaks an invariant it is **modelled as a type**, never exempted by matching a name string — ERPNext exempts "Exchange Gain Or Loss" by string in ≥5 places (PHASE2 §5).

**The party subledger — the third ledger, which revision 2 named in step 9 and never defined.** A1, A2, A5 and A7 are among the most expensive rules in §6.1 and none of them was writable against the document as it stood: "outstanding is derived by recomputation over an append-only signed subledger" is not a query anyone can write without the table.

```sql
create table payment_ledger_entry (
  id uuid primary key,
  seq bigint generated always as identity unique,          -- ordering, as in gl_entry
  posting_date date not null, document_date date not null,
  company_id uuid not null, registration_id uuid not null,
  party_type text not null, party_id uuid not null,
  account_id uuid not null references account(id),
  voucher_type text not null, voucher_id uuid not null, voucher_line_id uuid,
  against_voucher_type text, against_voucher_id uuid,      -- null on the invoice leg
  amount numeric(18,4) not null,                           -- SIGNED, company currency
  amount_ac_ccy numeric(18,4) not null, account_currency text not null,
  due_date date, installment_seq smallint,                 -- A3/A4 read these
  ruleset_version_id uuid not null,
  created_at timestamptz not null, created_by uuid not null
);
create index on payment_ledger_entry (party_type, party_id, account_id, posting_date, seq);
create index on payment_ledger_entry (voucher_type, voucher_id);

create table reconciliation_edge (                          -- A2: partial reconcile IS an edge
  id uuid primary key,
  debit_ple_id uuid not null references payment_ledger_entry(id),
  credit_ple_id uuid not null references payment_ledger_entry(id),
  amount numeric(18,4) not null check (amount > 0),         -- company currency
  created_at timestamptz not null, created_by uuid not null,
  unique (debit_ple_id, credit_ple_id)
);
```

Both tables are INSERT-only for the application role, exactly like `gl_entry` — with **one deliberate exception**: unreconciliation is `DELETE` on `reconciliation_edge`, performed by a named role, audited with a reason, which is what makes A2's "unreconcile is edge deletion" trivially correct instead of a compensating-entry problem. The edge is a *link*, not a posting, so deleting it moves no money. Outstanding per voucher is one CTE over signed `amount` minus the edges touching it; ageing by instalment is the same CTE grouped by `installment_seq` and `due_date`. Both are written **once**, in `packages/ledger`, and every report and every screen calls them — A1's failure mode is two implementations that disagree, not one that is wrong.

**The balance invariant is authoritative in the database.** The application check is fast feedback, not the guarantee. The guarantee is a deferred constraint trigger, adapted from Odoo Bet 2's `GROUP BY … HAVING ROUND(SUM(balance)) != 0` but promoted from an application contextmanager to a DB constraint:

```sql
create constraint trigger gl_voucher_balanced after insert on gl_entry
  deferrable initially deferred for each row execute function assert_voucher_balanced();
```

**Deduplication is required, not optional.** Postgres `CREATE CONSTRAINT TRIGGER` supports only `FOR EACH ROW` — a statement-level constraint trigger with a transition table is not available, so the reviewer's preferred fix cannot be taken. Left naive, a 6-leg voucher runs the balancing aggregate six times and §3.8's ~1000-row import batches run thousands of aggregate scans at COMMIT. The trigger function therefore **dedupes by `voucher_id` in a transaction-local set** (a `pg_temp` table or an array in a session variable, cleared at commit) and performs one aggregate per voucher per transaction. This is a correctness-neutral, mandatory optimisation and it belongs in the same migration as the trigger.

**And the deferred trigger must be reconciled with the savepoint-per-batch import design, which revision 2 left in conflict.** A deferred constraint trigger fires at COMMIT, **not at savepoint release** — so §3.8's "batches of ~1000 with a savepoint per batch" would detect an imbalanced batch only at the end of the whole transaction and roll back every earlier batch, defeating the savepoint entirely, while the pending-event queue for a 200k-row opening import is itself a memory problem. Required: the importer issues **`SET CONSTRAINTS gl_voucher_balanced IMMEDIATE`** at the end of each batch, so the assertion boundary and the savepoint boundary coincide and a bad batch fails alone, then re-defers for the next batch. This is one line in the import loop and it is the difference between a resumable import and an all-or-nothing one.

Nothing — not a bulk import, not a repost, not psql at 2am — can commit an unbalanced voucher. Third layer: a nightly auditor summing the ledger per (company, FY) that alarms on drift.

**Balances are derived. Always.** frappe-books proves it (PHASE6 §2: `Account.json` has no balance field; `isDebit`/`isCredit` derive from `rootType`); ERPNext confirms it (PHASE2 §3.1: outstanding is derived, never incremented). Cached read models — `account_period_balance`, `invoice_outstanding` — are permitted only if they are (1) rebuildable by one command, (2) stamped with a **safe watermark**, and (3) **never read by any posting-path validation**. Each gets a `recalculate` command and a divergence alarm — alarm, not silent repair (PHASE3 §6.6: "tolerating duplicates for concurrency is defensible — silently repairing without alarming is not").

**The watermark rule, stated once and applied everywhere.** `bigint generated always as identity` allocates on *request*, not on commit, so rows commit out of `seq` order and a naive "built up to seq N" cache permanently skips any row with a lower seq that committed later. Every incremental rebuild — caches here, the audit sealer in §3.5, the GSTR builders — advances its watermark only to `min(backend_xmin)` from `pg_stat_activity` (equivalently, the oldest snapshot still in flight), never to `max(seq)`. Revision 1 stated the high-water-mark design without this rule, which made it unsound.

**One company, one chart of accounts; registration is a dimension. [VERIFY] q81.** `gl_entry` carries both `company_id` and `registration_id` NOT NULL and revision 2 never said which of the two owns what — which matters because the stated ICP is 1–3 GSTINs and N16 makes a transfer between two of the same entity's registrations a *supply* requiring an invoice and an e-way bill. Fixed here: the **chart of accounts, the fiscal year, the accounting-period lock, `accounts_frozen_till` and the Period Closing Voucher are per company**; **numbering, the GSTR-1-filed lock, return periods, place-of-supply and every GST report are per registration**. A branch-transfer invoice is a real invoice with real GL on both sides, posted to inter-branch control accounts that net to zero at company level, flagged `is_internal_supply`, and **excluded by an explicit report rule from consolidated revenue, from consolidated COGS and from aggregate turnover** — not by a filter someone remembers to apply. Without the elimination the customer's turnover is overstated by every transfer, which then feeds the very turnover thresholds §5.2 depends on.

**`stock_balance` is not a cache.** It is a posting-path authority, locked and read inside the posting transaction (see below). The invariant above is amended to say so explicitly, because V6's negative-stock check, V11's availability formula and moving-average valuation all read a balance on the posting path and revision 1's blanket "no cache is read by posting-path validation" contradicted three of its own named rules.

**Valuation.** Movement-only SLE + a **separate append-only valuation layer** — combining PHASE2 §6's exit and PHASE3 §7 Q1's recommendation of the pre-Odoo-19 `stock.valuation.layer`. Odoo 19 deleted it for recompute-on-read, which is O(history); PHASE3 §6.17 calls that a cautionary tale. **Moving average only** in v1 (PHASE2 Q1 calls back-dating policy "the single highest-leverage decision in the whole report"): MA makes forward replay tractable, and FIFO bin queues plus dependant-SLE chains are the expensive version. *Revision 1 also argued "MA is Tally's default which reduces migration friction" — **that claim has no source in the scout** and is struck. The tractability argument stands on its own; the Tally default is a **[VERIFY] q74** to be confirmed against a real Tally export at Stage 0, since it materially affects opening-stock valuation.* FIFO mechanics are specified in the backlog (V5) for v1.1.

**Back-dating, decided on day one — and one precedence order, because revision 2 defined three overlapping windows and never ranked them.** The windows are `stock_frozen_upto` per warehouse, the open unfiled period, and the four period gates. They are evaluated in this order and the **first refusal wins**; there is no window that overrides another:

| Order | Window | Applies to | Who may move it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Period Closing Voucher / `accounts_frozen_till` | GL **and** stock | Nobody. Administrator is explicitly denied; reversing a PCV is a document, not a setting |
| 2 | GSTR-1/3B filed lock on `(GSTIN, period)` | GL and any document with a `gst_report_period` | Nobody; a filed period is un-marked only by the audited supervisor action described above |
| 3 | Accounting-period document lock | GL | The named exempted role, audited |
| 4 | `stock_frozen_upto` (default T−7, per warehouse) | Stock only | Warehouse supervisor, audited, forward-only within windows 1–3 |

The only entry that passes 1 and 2 is the `deemed_supply_143` voucher type, and it does so *without back-dating* — see below. Permitted back-dating is therefore **only within the open, unfiled period**, and each warehouse carries `stock_frozen_upto` (default today − 7 days). Inside that window a back-dated entry enqueues a bounded, checkpointed, resumable revaluation over the affected `(item, warehouse)` from that `seq` forward. Outside it, correction is a **forward-dated adjustment document**, never an edit or a repost. This deletes ERPNext's unbounded repost subsystem — the one PHASE2 documents sitting Queued for hours and blocking period close.

**The repost is *observable* as well as bounded, and revision 5 makes that a stated obligation rather than an implementation detail — because §8.4 answer 13 sells against exactly this failure.** ERPNext's back-dated revaluation reposting has been reported stuck in queue for months across three major versions, with one user reverting to manual accounting *"in order to avoid accounting errors and losses"* and another reporting **74,614 items queued and twelve months of incorrect item valuation on a 16-core / 32 GB server running v15** **[PHASE8 VERIFIED — discuss.frappe.io, first-party, dated, queue figure and hardware confirmed across posts in the thread]**. If we say our repost is bounded and observable in a sales call, then it ships with: a **visible queue depth and per-run progress**, a **stated completion SLA** with an alarm when a run exceeds it, and a **refusal to close a period while a repost affecting it is outstanding** — the failure must be loud rather than silent. **The claim in §8.4 answer 13 is a lie unless these exist**, which is this document's own rule that a capability named in a sales line appears in a stage: it is part of Stage 3's bounded-repost deliverable.

**The §143 deemed-supply invoice does not back-date, and revision 1's claim that it does was both unsourced and structurally impossible.** Revision 1 said it "by statute bears the original despatch date" — stated flat, while §5.5 q24–33 admits "we assume back-dated to original despatch — confirm" and PHASE4 §5.4 marks the whole of §143 **[NOT IN CODE]**. Worse, a 1-year (inputs) or 3-year (capital goods) expiry routinely lands the despatch date in a **closed prior FY** whose series is exhausted, whose continuity has been asserted, whose period is frozen and whose GSTR-1 is filed — and an IRP will not accept a document dated years back (§5.5 q51). The mechanism, corrected:

> Current-FY series, **current posting date**, `supply_date = original despatch date` as a stored field, `late_disclosed = true`, voucher type `deemed_supply_143`. Interest computes from `supply_date`; the GL and the return land in the current open period. **`gst_report_period` is a stored field set at post time and distinct from `posting_date`** — the GSTR-1/3B builders read it, so no document can silently land in a filed period's return. **[VERIFY] q24–33** governs the date the invoice must actually bear; if the CA's answer is that it must bear the despatch date, that is a print-layout and disclosure question over `supply_date`, not a posting-date question.

### 3.5 Audit trail

PHASE5 §5.2 lists **eight independently verified gaps in Frappe's `Version`, six of which it grades Fatal** — revision 1 said "fails six of eight Rule 11(g) criteria", which invented an eight-criterion checklist that exists nowhere in the scout. Minor in substance, material in discipline: this document's own standard is that every load-bearing claim cites its report. PHASE4 §7 says the mandate constrains the core data layer and cannot be retrofitted.

```sql
create table audit_log (
  seq bigint generated always as identity,
  occurred_at timestamptz not null default clock_timestamp(),
  txid xid8 not null default pg_current_xact_id(),
  event_id uuid not null,        -- ONE business operation; parent + N child rows share it
  root_entity text not null, root_entity_id uuid not null,   -- the document the event is about
  entity text not null, entity_id uuid not null, doc_no text,
  action text not null,          -- insert|update|submit|cancel|amend|delete|config_change
  actor_user_id uuid not null, actor_roles text[] not null,
  impersonated_by uuid, api_key_id uuid, session_id uuid,
  client_ip inet, user_agent text, request_id text not null, app_version text not null,
  company_id uuid not null,      -- PHASE4 §7.5: the reference has no company stamp
  before jsonb, after jsonb, changed_fields text[],
  reason text,                   -- mandatory for cancel / amend / config_change
  chain_seq bigint not null,     -- per-tenant, allocated under the chain-head lock
  prev_hash bytea not null, row_hash bytea not null,  -- row_hash covers prev_hash
  primary key (occurred_at, seq)  -- a PK on a partitioned table MUST include the key
) partition by range (occurred_at);
create index on audit_log (event_id);
create index on audit_log (root_entity, root_entity_id, occurred_at);
create unique index on audit_log (chain_seq);   -- per tenant schema; what audit:verify walks
```

*Two DDL corrections the second review round found.* Revision 2 wrote `seq … primary key` on a table declared `partition by range (occurred_at)` — **invalid Postgres**, because a unique constraint on a partitioned table must contain the partition key; the migration would not have applied. And `txid_current()` returns a wrapping 32-bit value and forces XID assignment on transactions that would otherwise be read-only; `pg_current_xact_id()` returning `xid8` is the current spelling and does not wrap.

`event_id` and `root_entity` exist because §4.1 requires field-level deltas **including child rows** and a per-change-*event* reporting view. Revision 1 keyed the table on `(entity, entity_id)` alone, so an invoice submit wrote one parent row and N line rows with nothing to group them into an event — and `txid` cannot do it, because a batch job writes hundreds of documents under one txid.

Seven required properties:

1. **Snapshot at insert** (`before = null, after = <full row>`). Frappe's `for_insert()` returning false unless a data-import flag is set is its single worst gap — without a baseline the trail is unreadable and you must replay diffs backwards.
2. **Actor inside the payload** — user, roles at the time, impersonation chain, API key, session, IP, UA, trace id. Never inferred from the row's `owner` column.
3. **Append-only at the database level.** `REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE ON audit_log FROM app_role`, plus a `BEFORE UPDATE OR DELETE` trigger that raises unconditionally so even the table owner cannot quietly edit history. Retention is a *separate* role that may only `DETACH PARTITION`, and the detach is itself logged.
4. **No cascade.** Deleting a draft appends a deletion record with the complete final snapshot. Submitted documents are never deleted; there is no code path that removes an `audit_log` row.
5. **Hash chain, computed inline at insert — not by a sealer.** Revision 1 was internally contradictory and would not have built: property 3 revokes UPDATE and adds a trigger that raises *unconditionally*, and property 5 then had a background sealer *setting* `prev_hash`, which is an UPDATE. Two further defects in the same design: `row_hash` was `not null` at insert and so could not cover a `prev_hash` written later — making the "chain" a list of independent digests with no more tamper-evidence than the daily digest already provides; and `seq` gaps on every rolled-back transaction, so `audit:verify` would alarm on legitimate holes.

   Corrected: each tenant has an `audit_chain_head(tenant_id, chain_seq, head_hash)` row. The audit writer takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock` on **that row's key** (never on `audit_log`), reads `head_hash`, computes `row_hash = H(prev_hash || canonical(row))`, allocates `chain_seq = head + 1`, inserts, and updates the head. The lock is held from allocation to COMMIT — like every row lock in Postgres, and like the series lock in §3.6 — which is why both are taken in the same slot at the very end of the transaction with nothing but the insert and the commit behind them. "Microseconds" was revision 2's claim and it was wrong about the mechanism; what is true is that the *work* under the lock is one hash and one update, so the hold time is bounded by commit latency and not by anything the application does. **`chain_seq`, not `seq`, is what `audit:verify` walks** — it is gapless by construction because it is allocated inside the successful transaction; `seq` remains a physical identity with expected gaps and `audit:verify` ignores it. On top: a **daily digest** `(date, first_chain_seq, last_chain_seq, head_hash)`, signed, written to an external append-only store and mailed to the tenant's nominated CA.

   We ship both because PHASE5's open question 3 (does the auditor expect a per-row chain, or do append-only grants plus a signed digest suffice?) is unanswered — but the fallback is now real: **if §10 Q5 says the digest suffices, drop the chain columns and the head table entirely** and keep append-only grants + signed daily digest. What we do not do is ship belt-and-braces where the braces require violating the belt.
6. **Mandatory, not opt-in — and there is no enable flag.** The writer lives in the repository layer, fires for every write of every entity, with no per-entity toggle and no `ignore_version` flag in the codebase. Revision 2 kept india-compliance's **one-way tenant enable flag** alongside the word "mandatory", which is a contradiction with a statutory consequence: Rule 11(g) asks whether the facility was *operated throughout the year for all transactions*, so a tenant able to run half a year with the log off earns a qualified report from a product sold on compliance. **The flag is deleted.** What survives from PHASE4 §7.2/§7.4 is the pair of locks that were the useful half: a **configuration lock** inspecting both pre-save and post-save state so a document type cannot be swapped out of protection, and a **retention lock** permanently disabling any purge feature. The config record itself is audited.

   **The importer is not a bypass, and this is the clause the whole Rule 11(g) argument rests on.** PHASE4 §7.6 names it as the reference's defect — "anything running during install or migrate bypasses every guard" — and revision 2 then routed bulk work through a separate migrator process without ever saying that the migrator writes through the audited repository. It does. The import engine, the Tally bridge, the opening-balance loader and every repost call the same chokepoint with `actor_user_id` = the operator who started the run, `action` = the real action, and a `reason` naming the import job; a migration run appears in the trail as a bounded set of events, not as an absence. **A test asserts that a row written by the migrator produces an `audit_log` row** — if that test is deleted, the compliance claim goes with it. `psql` at 2am is the only remaining hole and the append-only grants plus the digest are what cover it.
7. **Partitioned from day one** (monthly range), never deleted, archived to cold storage on a schedule read from the rule store. **Retention is not a constant.** Revision 2 asserted 8 years as settled while §5.5 q66 lists the retention period as an open question, and GST s.36 (understood as 72 months from the annual-return due date, longer where an appeal or investigation is pending) and the income-tax reassessment window can each outrun a flat figure. Retention is therefore a **rule-store row per record class** (**[VERIFY] q76**) with a documented floor of the longest applicable statute, and `audit:verify` must run over **detached and archived partitions too** — a chain that verifies only over hot data proves nothing about the years an assessment actually reaches. `audit_log` will dwarf the ledgers; Frappe's answer is "delete it", which directly contradicts statutory retention.

Rich-text and JSONB fields are diffed too — Frappe blacklists four fieldtypes from diffing, which is material for terms and notes on statutory documents. **Coverage: every write of every entity**, which is what property 6 says and what revision 1's narrower "GL/stock-affecting documents plus the config record" sentence contradicted. **Masters are explicitly in scope** — a party GSTIN, an item tax template, an item HSN or an Address state change alters filed returns and produces no GL effect at the moment of the edit. The named list (PHASE4 §7.3) is a floor: Stock Entry, Stock Reconciliation, Subcontracting Challan and Receipt, Landed Cost Voucher, Bill of Entry, plus Party, Address, Item, Item Tax Template, GSTIN Registration and the audit config record. **Reporting views required for the CA conversation:** per-change-*event* detail keyed on `event_id` (never collapsed per document — the reference collapses on last-modified timestamp and is therefore useless), summary by document type, summary by user; company and date-window filters mandatory. Plus `audit:verify --from --to`, which walks `chain_seq` and exits non-zero on any break.

**Rule 11(g) at the obligation level, not just the row level.** The log above is stronger than anything in the references; what revision 1 omitted is everything around it, and an auditor qualifies the report on any of these. All four are **[VERIFY] q75–q78** — design commitments the product makes cheaply, not statements of law:

| # | Obligation | What the product does |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Books in electronic mode kept on servers **physically located in India**, daily backup, accessible in India at all times (Companies (Accounts) Rules, Rule 3) | India-region hosting as a contractual *and* schema-level fact: region stamped on the tenant record, on every daily digest and on the cold-storage location. "Data residency" appeared once in revision 1 — as a GSP *sales objection* |
| 2 | Annual intimation to the ROC of the service provider's name, IP address, location and address where books are kept (Rule 3(6)) | A generated **"Rule 3(6) disclosure pack"**. One page, no competitor ships it, and it is a sales artefact as much as a compliance one |
| 3 | On-prem hands the customer a Postgres superuser who can `ALTER TABLE … DISABLE TRIGGER` and re-chain the log at leisure | For on-prem, **outbound digest anchoring is mandatory, not optional**, with a hard-fail alarm after N days of silence. No anchoring, no Rule 11(g) claim in the sales deck (§3.11) |
| 4 | Personal data under the **DPDP Act 2023** sits inside a log the plan says is never deleted | Crypto-shredding for personal data, immutability for statutory records — §3.11, and **D14** in §9.1 |

### 3.6 Numbering

```sql
create table document_series (
  id uuid primary key,
  registration_id uuid not null references gstin_registration(id),
  doc_type text not null,
  series_code text not null,           -- 'PLANT', 'DEPOT', 'EXP', 'GATE'
  series_nature text not null,         -- tax_invoice|bill_of_supply|credit_note|
                                       -- debit_note|delivery_challan|receipt_voucher|
                                       -- payment_voucher|refund_voucher|revised_invoice
  fiscal_year_id uuid not null,
  format text not null,                -- 'INV/{GSTIN_STATE}/{FY}/{#####}'
  start_ordinal bigint not null default 1,   -- first ordinal THIS system issues
  current bigint not null default 0,
  migrated_from bigint, migrated_to bigint,  -- externally-issued range, declared not enumerated
  migration_note text,                       -- provenance: source system, cut-over date, who
  unique (registration_id, doc_type, series_code, fiscal_year_id)
);

-- Uniqueness of the number itself. Revision 2 enforced gaplessness with a nightly
-- auditor and uniqueness with nothing at all.
create unique index on <document_table> (registration_id, doc_type, doc_no)
  where doc_no is not null;
```

That partial unique index goes on **every** document table carrying a statutory series. The counter is what makes numbers gapless; the index is what makes them unique, and the two are different guarantees — a bug in the allocator, a restored backup or a hand-run `UPDATE` produces a duplicate that the continuity auditor, which only checks for holes, will not see.

Scope is the quadruple **(GSTIN registration, document type, series code, Indian FY 1 Apr–31 Mar)**.

**Two corrections revision 1 needed, both found by tracing a real mid-year migration.**

*One series per GSTIN per year was wrong.* Revision 1's unique key permitted exactly one invoice series per GSTIN per FY.
